The method's simplicity and efficiency are particularly noteworthy. It provides a good foundation for further research and development in this important area.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safer Skin Lesion Classification with Global Class Activation Probability Map Evaluation and SafeML
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20776v1)
- **Authors**: Kuniko Paxton, Koorosh Aslansefat, Amila Akagić, Dhavalkumar Thakker, Yiannis Papadopoulos
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in skin lesion classification models have significantly improved accuracy, with some models even surpassing dermatologists' diagnostic performance. However, in medical practice, distrust in AI models remains a challenge. Beyond high accuracy, trustworthy, explainable diagnoses are essential. Existing explainability methods have reliability issues, with LIME-based methods suffering from inconsistency, while CAM-based methods failing to consider all classes. To address these limitations, we propose Global Class Activation Probabilistic Map Evaluation, a method that analyses all classes' activation probability maps probabilistically and at a pixel level. By visualizing the diagnostic process in a unified manner, it helps reduce the risk of misdiagnosis. Furthermore, the application of SafeML enhances the detection of false diagnoses and issues warnings to doctors and patients as needed, improving diagnostic reliability and ultimately patient safety. We evaluated our method using the ISIC datasets with MobileNetV2 and Vision Transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called "Global Class Activation Probability Map Evaluation (GCAPM)" integrated with SafeML to enhance the reliability and safety of skin lesion classification models. GCAPM analyzes activation probability maps probabilistically at a pixel level across all classes to visualize the diagnostic process in a unified manner, aiming to reduce misdiagnosis. SafeML is incorporated to detect false diagnoses and issue warnings. The method is evaluated on ISIC datasets using MobileNet V2 and Vision Transformers. The authors argue that their approach improves explainability compared to conventional CAM methods and enhances the detection of abnormal diagnoses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Critical Problem:** The paper tackles a vital issue in medical AI: the lack of trust in models due to their "black box" nature and the potential for misdiagnosis even with high accuracy. Explainability is key to clinical adoption, and the paper attempts to improve it.
*   **Novelty in Approach:** GCAPM provides a more holistic analysis of class activation maps compared to traditional CAM-based methods that focus solely on the predicted class. This broader view could potentially reveal insights missed by other techniques. Integrating this with SafeML principles to detect and flag potentially unreliable predictions during runtime is also innovative.
*   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The use of Attribute Sensitivity and Attribute False Positive Rate to quantify the quality of explainability and connect it with prediction accuracy is a good step towards making the evaluation of explainable AI more rigorous. The analysis of these metrics within and outside confidence intervals is valuable.
*   **Practical Application:** The integration of SafeML principles for runtime monitoring of data drift and flagging uncertain predictions improves diagnostic reliability, especially in self-diagnosis scenarios. This has potential for wider clinical use.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The method is tested on standard datasets (ISIC 2017 and 2019) using common architectures (MobileNet V2, ViT), providing a degree of comparability with other research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Base Components:** While the GCAPM *evaluation* is novel, it relies heavily on existing Grad-CAM techniques. The "innovation" hinges more on the *analysis* of all class activations rather than a complete re-invention of activation map generation. The SafeML aspects are also built on existing concepts of data drift detection, adapted to the context of image analysis.
*   **Complexity and Interpretability of GCAPM Visualizations:** While offering more information, GCAPM's all-class visualization might be more complex to interpret for clinicians compared to simpler CAMs focusing on the predicted class. The paper doesn't fully address how clinicians would integrate this richer information into their decision-making process practically. This might be hard to adapt in medical practices if doctors are unfamiliar with AI-explanations.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The simulated data drift (Gaussian blur) is a relatively simple and artificial type of data shift. Real-world data drifts are likely to be more complex and multifaceted, including variations in image quality, patient demographics, and lesion characteristics.
*   **Reliance on Lesion Segmentation:** The evaluation relies on accurate lesion segmentation masks. While the paper uses a pre-trained DeepLabV3 model, the accuracy of these segmentation masks can impact the attribute metrics, potentially introducing noise into the evaluation. ISIC 2019 relied on generated masks, a potential source of inaccuracy.
*   **Modest Improvements (Potentially):** The experimental results, while showing improvements, don't demonstrate groundbreaking or transformative advancements. The accuracy gains and the effectiveness of the selective prediction mechanism need to be assessed critically in real-world clinical trials.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a useful contribution to the area of explainable AI in medical imaging, specifically for skin lesion classification. However, its impact is somewhat limited by its incremental nature building upon existing techniques and somewhat limited scope of its real-world application.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable addition to the field of skin lesion classification, particularly in addressing the crucial issue of trust in AI-driven diagnoses. The GCAPM approach offers a novel way to analyze class activation maps, considering all classes and providing a potentially more nuanced understanding of the model's reasoning. Furthermore, the integration of SafeML principles to detect and flag potentially unreliable predictions during runtime is a step in the right direction. However, the impact is limited by its relative dependence on existing CAM techniques and a limited real-world clinical trial evaluation.
For these reasons, a score reflecting both its contributions and limitations is justified.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating Compositional Generalisation in VLMs and Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20783v1)
- **Authors**: Beth Pearson, Bilal Boulbarss, Michael Wray, Martha Lewis
- **Abstract**: A fundamental aspect of the semantics of natural language is that novel meanings can be formed from the composition of previously known parts. Vision-language models (VLMs) have made significant progress in recent years, however, there is evidence that they are unable to perform this kind of composition. For example, given an image of a red cube and a blue cylinder, a VLM such as CLIP is likely to incorrectly label the image as a red cylinder or a blue cube, indicating it represents the image as a `bag-of-words' and fails to capture compositional semantics. Diffusion models have recently gained significant attention for their impressive generative abilities, and zero-shot classifiers based on diffusion models have been shown to perform competitively with CLIP in certain compositional tasks. In this work we explore whether the generative Diffusion Classifier has improved compositional generalisation abilities compared to discriminative models. We assess three models -- Diffusion Classifier, CLIP, and ViLT -- on their ability to bind objects with attributes and relations in both zero-shot learning (ZSL) and generalised zero-shot learning (GZSL) settings. Our results show that the Diffusion Classifier and ViLT perform well at concept binding tasks, but that all models struggle significantly with the relational GZSL task, underscoring the broader challenges VLMs face with relational reasoning. Analysis of CLIP embeddings suggests that the difficulty may stem from overly similar representations of relational concepts such as left and right. Code and dataset are available at: https://github.com/otmive/diffusion_classifier_clip
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the compositional generalisation abilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Diffusion Models (specifically Diffusion Classifier) in binding attributes to objects and understanding relations between objects. It extends the Concept Binding Benchmark by Lewis et al. to include zero-shot learning (ZSL) and generalised zero-shot learning (GZSL) settings. The models (Diffusion Classifier, CLIP, and ViLT) are tested on their ability to bind colours to objects and understand spatial relations (left/right) between objects in single-object, two-object, and relational scenarios. The results show that while Diffusion Classifier performs well in single-object settings, ViLT excels in two-object tasks.  All models struggle with relational reasoning in the GZSL setting, failing to differentiate between 'left' and 'right' relations. The paper also includes an analysis of CLIP embeddings, suggesting that the difficulty with relational reasoning might stem from overly similar representations of relational concepts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comparative analysis of Diffusion Classifier *against* CLIP and ViLT on a compositional generalisation task *and* in its extension of the Concept Binding Benchmark to include GZSL *and* in its analysis of embedding spaces for relational reasoning. Comparing Diffusion Classifiers with discriminative VLMs is a relevant and timely area of research. Extending the existing benchmark and providing code & data availability further adds to the contribution. However, similar studies have been done on compositional generalisation and the method is quite direct, relying heavily on an existing experimental setup.

*   **Significance:** The findings underscore the broader challenges VLMs face with relational reasoning and compositional semantics. This confirms similar observations from other studies but offers a direct comparison of a recently popular Diffusion model based classifier to more established VLMs. The analysis of CLIP embeddings to explain the poor relational reasoning provides additional insight into the limitations of current VLMs, even when fine-tuned.  The investigation into different training approaches (positive examples only) is relevant, though a more in-depth ablation of training strategies would have further strengthened the paper. This may point to avenues for developing improved architectures or training strategies. The paper provides a useful comparative analysis which highlights the limitations of current VLMs on a task known to be difficult. This is an incremental step towards better understanding these limitations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined experimental setup.
    *   Rigorous comparison of three models (Diffusion Classifier, CLIP, and ViLT).
    *   Extension of an existing benchmark to include ZSL and GZSL.
    *   Analysis of CLIP embeddings to explain poor relational reasoning.
    *   Publicly available code and dataset.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited ablation studies (e.g., training strategies, architectures).
    *   Relatively simple synthetic datasets that may not fully capture the complexity of real-world scenes (acknowledged).
    *   Findings on relational reasoning confirm previous work.
    *   Although the paper explores fine-tuning, it doesn't provide an in-depth analysis of different fine-tuning approaches or their impact on compositional understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's contribution is incremental but worthwhile. It offers further evidence that VLMs still struggle with compositional reasoning and provides a direct comparison with Diffusion Model based classifiers, and contributes a publicly available benchmark. This contributes to a growing body of evidence regarding VLM limitations and will be useful for researchers interested in benchmarking new models for compositional abilities.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** While the paper addresses a relevant problem and includes a rigorous experimental setup, its findings largely confirm existing observations. The novelty is primarily in the direct comparison of Diffusion Classifiers alongside other VLMs and the extension of the benchmark to GZSL.  The paper offers a clear and valuable comparative analysis of existing models but doesn't introduce a breakthrough in methodology or understanding. Therefore, the score reflects the incremental, rather than transformative, nature of its contribution to the field.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Exploring Machine Learning and Language Models for Multimodal Depression Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20805v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Si Zhao Hong, Timothy Zoe Delaya, Sherwyn Chan Yin Kit, Pai Chet Ng, Xiaoxiao Miao
- **Abstract**: This paper presents our approach to the first Multimodal Personality-Aware Depression Detection Challenge, focusing on multimodal depression detection using machine learning and deep learning models. We explore and compare the performance of XGBoost, transformer-based architectures, and large language models (LLMs) on audio, video, and text features. Our results highlight the strengths and limitations of each type of model in capturing depression-related signals across modalities, offering insights into effective multimodal representation strategies for mental health prediction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of machine learning and language models for multimodal depression detection within the context of the first Multimodal Personality-Aware Depression Detection (MPDD) Challenge. It compares the performance of three model classes – XGBoost, transformer-based architectures, and Large Language Models (LLMs) – using audio, video, and text features from the MPDD dataset. The study evaluates the strengths and limitations of each model in capturing depression-related signals across modalities, aiming to identify effective multimodal representation strategies for mental health prediction. The experiments involve ablation studies, parameter tuning, and analysis of weighted/unweighted F1 scores on the MPDD-Elderly and MPDD-Young datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a systematic comparison of three distinct modeling approaches (XGBoost, Transformers, and LLMs) for depression detection, covering both traditional machine learning and modern deep learning techniques.
    *   **MPDD Challenge Focus:** The study aligns with a specific challenge (MPDD), providing practical insights into the performance of different models on a relatively new and richly annotated dataset.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies helps to identify the crucial components and configurations for each model, contributing to a deeper understanding of their performance.
    *   **Modality Fusion Strategies:** The paper explores different modalities and fusion strategies, which is essential for the effectiveness of multimodal depression detection.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured**: The paper is well-organized, and the experimental setup and results are clearly presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The paper primarily applies existing models to a new dataset. While valuable, it doesn't introduce fundamentally new methodologies or architectures. There is a high reliance on pre-trained models and existing techniques with limited innovation in combining these.
    *   **LLM Underperformance:** The results show that LLMs perform poorly compared to simpler models (XGBoost, Transformers) on this task. The paper does not provide an in-depth analysis to why this is the case beyond suggesting possible adaptation challenges. More investigation into LLM prompt engineering and fine-tuning strategies might be beneficial.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The datasets, while valuable, may still suffer from limitations regarding size and representativeness, especially in the context of real-world deployment. Further work might explore how these models generalize across different datasets and populations.
    *   **Lack of Interpretability:** While XGBoost offers some interpretability, transformer-based models and LLMs are still largely black boxes. The paper could benefit from discussing methods to interpret the decisions made by these models, which is crucial in a clinical setting.
    *   **Reproducibility Concerns:** While the paper details the experimental setup, providing access to the code and specific parameter settings would greatly improve reproducibility.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on automated depression detection.
    *   The systematic comparison of different models can inform the design of future depression detection systems.
    *   The use of a new dataset (MPDD) helps to advance the field by providing a benchmark for future research.
    *   The study provides insight into the strengths and weaknesses of different modeling approaches, guiding future research in this area.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper is a solid piece of applied research, providing a useful evaluation of different modeling approaches for multimodal depression detection. However, its lack of methodological novelty and limited investigation of LLM underperformance holds it back from being a truly significant contribution. The application of established techniques to a new dataset is valuable, but the paper doesn't offer groundbreaking insights or transformative innovations.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### cMALC-D: Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20818v1)
- **Authors**: Anirudh Satheesh, Keenan Powell, Hua Wei
- **Abstract**: Many multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms are trained in fixed simulation environments, making them brittle when deployed in real-world scenarios with more complex and uncertain conditions. Contextual MARL (cMARL) addresses this by parameterizing environments with context variables and training a context-agnostic policy that performs well across all environment configurations. Existing cMARL methods attempt to use curriculum learning to help train and evaluate context-agnostic policies, but they often rely on unreliable proxy signals, such as value estimates or generalized advantage estimates that are noisy and unstable in multi-agent settings due to inter-agent dynamics and partial observability. To address these issues, we propose Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending (cMALC-D), a framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate semantically meaningful curricula and provide a more robust evaluation signal. To prevent mode collapse and encourage exploration, we introduce a novel diversity-based context blending mechanism that creates new training scenarios by combining features from prior contexts. Experiments in traffic signal control domains demonstrate that cMALC-D significantly improves both generalization and sample efficiency compared to existing curriculum learning baselines. We provide code at https://github.com/DaRL-LibSignal/cMALC-D.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the cMALC-D paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending (cMALC-D), a novel framework for improving the generalization capabilities of multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) agents in contextual environments (cMARL). cMALC-D addresses limitations in existing cMARL curriculum learning methods that rely on unreliable proxy signals or static schedules. It leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate semantically meaningful curricula by reasoning over past training results and proposing new contexts. A diversity-based context blending mechanism prevents mode collapse and encourages exploration by mixing previously sampled contexts. The approach is evaluated on traffic signal control scenarios, demonstrating improved generalization and sample efficiency compared to baseline curriculum learning algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components:

*   **LLM-Guided Curriculum Generation:**  The use of LLMs to dynamically generate cMARL curricula is a significant departure from traditional methods relying on heuristics or static schedules. This allows for a more adaptable and semantically informed curriculum, potentially leading to more robust learning.
*   **Diversity-Based Context Blending:** This mechanism addresses a crucial limitation of curriculum learning: mode collapse. The blending process encourages exploration and prevents overfitting to specific task distributions, enhancing generalization.
*   **Combining LLMs with a MARL Algorithm:** The system uses the strengths of each technology (the LLM's planning abilities and a MARL algorithm's optimization abilities) to make a powerful solution.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Generalization in MARL:** Generalization is a persistent challenge in MARL, limiting the applicability of trained agents to real-world scenarios. cMALC-D directly addresses this issue, providing a potential solution for deploying MARL agents in complex, uncertain environments.
*   **Improving Sample Efficiency:** By leveraging LLMs for curriculum design, cMALC-D aims to improve sample efficiency compared to random sampling or hand-crafted curricula. This is particularly important in MARL, where training can be computationally expensive.
*   **Application to Traffic Signal Control:** The choice of traffic signal control as an evaluation domain is relevant and has real-world implications. The paper demonstrates the potential of cMALC-D to improve traffic flow and reduce congestion.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing cMARL curriculum learning methods.
*   **Well-Defined Approach:** cMALC-D is clearly described, with a detailed explanation of each component (LLM-guided context generation, diversity-based blending).
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough empirical evaluation of cMALC-D, comparing it against several strong baselines across multiple environments.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are essential for understanding the contribution of each component of the framework. The study of the diversity mechansim really proves its worth.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM Dependence:** The framework relies on the capabilities of LLMs, which can be computationally expensive and potentially sensitive to prompt design. The scalability of cMALC-D to larger, more complex MARL problems needs to be further investigated. This is somewhat negated by the LLM being compressed via quantization and offloaded to a 2080 GPU.
*   **Context Representation:** The success of cMALC-D depends on how well the context is represented. This context representation might not be applicable to other environments.
*   **Specific LLM Use**: It is not clear what is special about the Qwen2.5-7B model, or if the design decisions are particularly suited for this model. This may limit the generality of the method.

**Potential Influence:**

cMALC-D has the potential to influence the field of MARL by:

*   Inspiring new approaches to curriculum learning that leverage the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.
*   Providing a practical solution for improving the generalization of MARL agents in real-world applications.
*   Highlighting the importance of context diversity in curriculum design.
*   Inspiring new methods that combine LLM with MARL techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty and significance of the proposed framework, the thorough empirical evaluation, and the potential influence on the field, but also accounting for the limitations of dependency of LLMs and general context representation, I assign this paper a score of 7.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GDLLM: A Global Distance-aware Modeling Approach Based on Large Language Models for Event Temporal Relation Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20828v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Zhao, Wanting Ning, Yuxiao Fei, Yubo Feng, Lishuang Li
- **Abstract**: In Natural Language Processing(NLP), Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE) is to recognize the temporal relations of two events. Prior studies have noted the importance of language models for ETRE. However, the restricted pre-trained knowledge of Small Language Models(SLMs) limits their capability to handle minority class relations in imbalanced classification datasets. For Large Language Models(LLMs), researchers adopt manually designed prompts or instructions, which may introduce extra noise, leading to interference with the model's judgment of the long-distance dependencies between events. To address these issues, we propose GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach based on LLMs. We first present a distance-aware graph structure utilizing Graph Attention Network(GAT) to assist the LLMs in capturing long-distance dependency features. Additionally, we design a temporal feature learning paradigm based on soft inference to augment the identification of relations with a short-distance proximity band, which supplements the probabilistic information generated by LLMs into the multi-head attention mechanism. Since the global feature can be captured effectively, our framework substantially enhances the performance of minority relation classes and improves the overall learning ability. Experiments on two publicly available datasets, TB-Dense and MATRES, demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach based on Large Language Models (LLMs) for Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE). It tackles limitations of using Small Language Models (SLMs) and manually designed prompts for LLMs. GDLLM uses a distance-aware graph structure with Graph Attention Networks (GATs) to capture long-distance dependencies between events and designs a temporal feature learning paradigm based on soft inference to leverage probabilistic information from LLMs. Experiments on TB-Dense and MATRES datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing ideas (LLMs, GATs, soft inference) but presents a novel architecture, GDLLM, integrating them to specifically address the challenges in ETRE, particularly dealing with long-range dependencies and imbalanced datasets. Using probabilistic LLM outputs as edge features in a GAT is also somewhat novel. The zero-shot analysis is an interesting component.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in NLP: extracting temporal relations between events, which is essential for understanding narrative and reasoning. The performance improvements on benchmark datasets, especially for minority classes, are significant. By achieving SOTA without manually designed prompts/instructions and addressing data imbalances, the research contributes to more robust and generalizable ETRE systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple datasets and ablation studies.
    *   Clear explanation of the GDLLM architecture and its components.
    *   Addresses the limitations of SLMs and manually designed prompts/instructions.
    *   Effective integration of LLMs and GATs for capturing long-distance dependencies.
    *   Good results on minority categories.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The overall approach is an ensemble of existing ideas, although integrated and applied in a novel way.
    *   The improvements achieved are incremental rather than groundbreaking.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough error analysis.
    *   The limitations section mentions task diversity, but does not elaborate on ways to overcome it.
    *   The dependence on large LLMs, while providing performance benefits, might also raise concerns about computational cost and accessibility for resource-constrained environments. The paper does not offer concrete solutions for reducing these costs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach is likely to influence future research on ETRE, particularly by encouraging the use of LLMs with graph-based architectures and techniques to handle data imbalance. The soft inference learning paradigm can also inspire new methods for leveraging probabilistic information from LLMs.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a good combination of ideas and a well-engineered architecture that achieves strong results. The novelty lies in the specific integration of LLMs and GATs for ETRE with the soft inference technique. The significance is in improving performance, particularly on imbalanced datasets and without manual prompt engineering. However, the lack of groundbreaking theoretical contributions and incremental nature of the improvements prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Publish to Perish: Prompt Injection Attacks on LLM-Assisted Peer Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20863v2)
- **Authors**: Matteo Gioele Collu, Umberto Salviati, Roberto Confalonieri, Mauro Conti, Giovanni Apruzzese
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into the scientific peer-review process, raising new questions about their reliability and resilience to manipulation. In this work, we investigate the potential for hidden prompt injection attacks, where authors embed adversarial text within a paper's PDF to influence the LLM-generated review. We begin by formalising three distinct threat models that envision attackers with different motivations -- not all of which implying malicious intent. For each threat model, we design adversarial prompts that remain invisible to human readers yet can steer an LLM's output toward the author's desired outcome. Using a user study with domain scholars, we derive four representative reviewing prompts used to elicit peer reviews from LLMs. We then evaluate the robustness of our adversarial prompts across (i) different reviewing prompts, (ii) different commercial LLM-based systems, and (iii) different peer-reviewed papers. Our results show that adversarial prompts can reliably mislead the LLM, sometimes in ways that adversely affect a "honest-but-lazy" reviewer. Finally, we propose and empirically assess methods to reduce detectability of adversarial prompts under automated content checks.
- **Summary**: Here's a breakdown of what the prompt is asking for, and how it emphasizes a critical review:

**Key Requests & Expectations:**

*   **Concise Summary:** Briefly describe the paper's contents.
*   **Rigorous and Critical Evaluation (High Emphasis):** This is the core request. It's not just a summary or general impression, but a deep and detailed examination.  The word "rigorous" means thorough, precise, and adhering to strict standards. "Critical" implies scrutinizing and evaluating strengths and weaknesses objectively.
*   **Novelty and Significance:** Focus on how original and impactful the paper is within its field.
*   **Score (1-10):** Assign a numerical score based on novelty and significance.  This adds a quantifiable element to the evaluation.
*   **Critical Assessment:** The prompt explicitly commands the reviewer to be "critical" in their assessment.
*   **Well-Reasoned Arguments & Thorough Justification:**  The score must be supported by clear and logical arguments. A high-quality review is needed.  This isn't about gut feelings but about providing concrete reasons.
*   **Strengths and Weaknesses:** Discuss both positive and negative aspects of the paper.
*   **Potential Influence:** Consider the paper's potential long-term effects on the field.
*   **Score Format:** Use "Score: X" at the end to clearly indicate the numerical rating.
*   **Rigorous Rationale** The reviewer must provide a "rigorous rationale" for the assigned score, reinforcing the importance of thorough justification.

**Emphasis on a Critical and Thorough Review**

The prompt is constructed to discourage a superficial or overly positive review. The repeated use of words like "rigorous," "critical," and "thorough" signals that the reviewer is expected to be demanding and insightful. By specifically asking for well-reasoned arguments and the identification of weaknesses, the prompt promotes a balanced and objective evaluation.

In summary, this prompt aims to guide reviewers in providing comprehensive, critical, and justified evaluations of scholarly papers, emphasizing the importance of novelty, significance, and a clear rationale for the assigned score.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 0/10

### Deep Learning Framework for Early Detection of Pancreatic Cancer Using Multi-Modal Medical Imaging Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20877v1)
- **Authors**: Dennis Slobodzian, Karissa Tilbury, Amir Kordijazi
- **Abstract**: Pacreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) remains one of the most lethal forms of cancer, with a five-year survival rate below 10% primarily due to late detection. This research develops and validates a deep learning framework for early PDAC detection through analysis of dual-modality imaging: autofluorescence and second harmonic generation (SHG). We analyzed 40 unique patient samples to create a specialized neural network capable of distinguishing between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous tissue. Our methodology evaluated six distinct deep learning architectures, comparing traditional Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) with modern Vision Transformers (ViTs). Through systematic experimentation, we identified and overcome significant challenges in medical image analysis, including limited dataset size and class imbalance. The final optimized framework, based on a modified ResNet architecture with frozen pre-trained layers and class-weighted training, achieved over 90% accuracy in cancer detection. This represents a significant improvement over current manual analysis methods an demonstrates potential for clinical deployment. This work establishes a robust pipeline for automated PDAC detection that can augment pathologists' capabilities while providing a foundation for future expansion to other cancer types. The developed methodology also offers valuable insights for applying deep learning to limited-size medical imaging datasets, a common challenge in clinical applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper presents a deep learning framework for the early detection of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) using dual-modality medical imaging (autofluorescence and second harmonic generation or SHG). The study analyzes 40 patient samples to train a neural network capable of distinguishing between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous tissue. The methodology explores six deep learning architectures, comparing convolutional neural networks (CNNs) with vision transformers (ViTs). The authors address common challenges in medical image analysis such as limited dataset size and class imbalance by freezing pre-trained layers with ResNet architectures and implementing class-weighted training. The optimized framework achieves over 90% accuracy in cancer detection, which the authors claim is a significant improvement over current manual methods. The paper establishes a robust pipeline for automated PDAC detection and provides a foundation for future expansion to other cancer types and insights for applying deep learning to limited-size medical imaging datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clinically Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a significant clinical problem – the early detection of PDAC, which is often diagnosed late, leading to poor survival rates.
*   **Multi-Modal Approach:** Using a combination of autofluorescence and SHG imaging is potentially valuable, as these modalities provide complementary information about tissue microstructure and metabolic activity. The use of multiple imaging modes can potentially enhance the robustness of the findings
*   **Systematic Approach:** The authors perform a comprehensive comparison of different deep learning architectures (CNNs and ViTs), systematically addressing challenges like overfitting and class imbalance. The paper presents a thorough comparison of both approaches, as well as the implementation of ResNet
*   **Addressing Data Limitations:** The paper directly tackles the common issue of limited data in medical image analysis, using techniques such as transfer learning and data augmentation. This is a practical and relevant approach.
*   **Model Interpretability:** The use of Grad-CAM visualization is a valuable addition, providing insights into the model's decision-making process and potentially increasing clinician trust.
*   **High Accuracy:** The reported accuracy of over 90% is promising and suggests the potential for clinical utility.
*   **Data Availability:** Providing the code in a public repository enhances reproducibility and allows others to build upon the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Small Dataset:** The use of only 40 patient samples raises concerns about the generalizability of the results.  Even with data augmentation, the model might still be overfitting to the specific characteristics of this limited dataset.
*   **Single-Center Data:** Data from a single institution may introduce bias related to patient population, imaging protocols, and pathologist practices. External validation with data from other centers is crucial.
*   **Lack of Prospective Validation:** The study is retrospective, meaning the data was collected before the model was developed. Prospective validation is needed to assess the model's performance in real-world clinical settings.
*   **Limited Comparison to Existing Methods:** While the authors claim improvement over manual analysis, they do not provide a direct, quantitative comparison to the performance of experienced pathologists using traditional methods on the same dataset. Also, a direct comparison to other deep learning methods for pancreatic cancer detection would be beneficial.
*   **Potential Bias:** The study might be susceptible to data bias and human annotation bias because of the relatively small size of the dataset, and the likelihood of introducing biases through the segmentation of the cancerous regions.
*   **Vague Description of "Manual Analysis"**: The paper claims improvement over current "manual analysis methods" but does not sufficiently specify what is involved in such methods to allow for an understanding of what improvements this study is making.

**Novelty and Significance:**

While the paper addresses an important problem and utilizes relevant techniques, its overall novelty is moderate. Deep learning for cancer detection, and even for PDAC detection, is not entirely new. Multi-modal medical imaging is also an established concept.

The *significance* lies in the specific application of deep learning to a particular combination of imaging modalities (autofluorescence and SHG) for PDAC and the efforts to address challenges unique to medical imaging data (limited data, class imbalance). The focus on model interpretability is also a positive contribution. However, the limitations related to dataset size and validation detract from the overall significance. The implementation of feature freezing and class-balancing methods are commendable.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6** due to a strong focus on relevant techniques, and the systematic evaluation of data. The study is limited by the small dataset and lack of external/prospective validation and a lack of direct comparision with existing DL approaches. Further validation with external datasets and broader assessment of current practices would increase the value.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Understanding and evaluating computer vision models through the lens of counterfactuals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20881v1)
- **Authors**: Pushkar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Counterfactual reasoning -- the practice of asking ``what if'' by varying inputs and observing changes in model behavior -- has become central to interpretable and fair AI. This thesis develops frameworks that use counterfactuals to explain, audit, and mitigate bias in vision classifiers and generative models. By systematically altering semantically meaningful attributes while holding others fixed, these methods uncover spurious correlations, probe causal dependencies, and help build more robust systems. The first part addresses vision classifiers. CAVLI integrates attribution (LIME) with concept-level analysis (TCAV) to quantify how strongly decisions rely on human-interpretable concepts. With localized heatmaps and a Concept Dependency Score, CAVLI shows when models depend on irrelevant cues like backgrounds. Extending this, ASAC introduces adversarial counterfactuals that perturb protected attributes while preserving semantics. Through curriculum learning, ASAC fine-tunes biased models for improved fairness and accuracy while avoiding stereotype-laden artifacts. The second part targets generative Text-to-Image (TTI) models. TIBET provides a scalable pipeline for evaluating prompt-sensitive biases by varying identity-related terms, enabling causal auditing of how race, gender, and age affect image generation. To capture interactions, BiasConnect builds causal graphs diagnosing intersectional biases. Finally, InterMit offers a modular, training-free algorithm that mitigates intersectional bias via causal sensitivity scores and user-defined fairness goals. Together, these contributions show counterfactuals as a unifying lens for interpretability, fairness, and causality in both discriminative and generative models, establishing principled, scalable methods for socially responsible bias evaluation and mitigation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided PhD thesis outline:

**Summary:**

The thesis "Understanding and Evaluating Computer Vision Models Through the Lens of Counterfactuals" addresses the growing concern of bias, lack of interpretability, and lack of robustness in computer vision (CV) models. It leverages counterfactual reasoning—systematically exploring "what if" scenarios—as a central tool for explainability, fairness auditing, and bias mitigation. The thesis is divided into two main parts: one focusing on vision classifiers and the other on text-to-image (TTI) generative models.

For vision classifiers, the thesis proposes two new methods: CAVLI, which quantifies the influence of visual concepts on model decisions using attribution-based explanations and concept-level representations, and ASAC, a technique that generates adversarial counterfactuals preserving visual semantics while perturbing protected attributes, to improve fairness in CV classifiers.

For generative models, the thesis introduces TIBET, a framework for dynamically evaluating prompt-sensitive biases in TTI models by systematically varying identity-related terms in input prompts. Furthermore, it proposes BiasConnect and BiasGraph, which uses a causal graph-based diagnostic tool that measures intersectional effects between social attributes. Finally, it presents InterMit, a training-free modular algorithm that mitigates intersectional bias using causal sensitivity scores and user-defined fairness goals.

The thesis claims these contributions will advance fairness, interpretability, and causal reasoning in modern CV systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant and Timely Topic:** The thesis addresses a crucial and growing concern in AI: the ethical implications of biased, opaque, and brittle models. Fairness, explainability, and robustness are all hot topics in the field.
*   **Strong Central Theme:** Counterfactual reasoning is consistently applied as a core methodology, providing a unifying thread across diverse tasks and models.
*   **Novel Methodologies:** The thesis proposes several original methods (CAVLI, ASAC, TIBET, BiasConnect, InterMit) with the potential to improve model interpretability and fairness in different contexts.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The thesis addresses both discriminative classifiers and generative models, offering a broad perspective on the challenges and solutions for ethical AI.
*   **Intersectional Focus:** Recognizing and tackling intersectional biases is a significant contribution, as many existing approaches handle biases in isolation.
*   **Practical Applications:** The thesis is geared toward practical utility, offering actionable tools for both auditing and mitigating biases in real-world systems.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Assumptions:** While counterfactuals offer many advantages, they depend on the nature of the data and it can be challenging to generate realistic, plausible counterfactuals.
*   **Generalizability/Scalability:** The complexity of developing VQA systems for generating image concepts in the described approaches limit the scalability and generalizability to other complex datasets.
*   **Subjectivity in Evaluation:** Fairness is inherently subjective. While the thesis aims for objectivity, the choice of metrics, the definition of "ideal" distributions, and the reliance on human judgments in some evaluations can introduce biases.
*   **Complexity and Integration:** The individual components like bias detection, visual heatmaps, and curriculum-based learning, in the described framework are not well-integrated, and can be very complex and task specific in nature.
*   **Lack of real-world validation**: Most evaluations and metrics seem to be measured on synthetic datasets, and the long-term effects of applying such mitigation techniques on real world data cannot be quantified.
*   **Reliance on downstream systems:** The accuracy of the TTI models as well as VQA models for obtaining image concepts has a significant influence on the quality and generalizability of the described approaches.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The thesis presents a solid set of contributions in a very active area of research. The introduction of CAVLI is novel and tackles an important problem. The ASAC method appears to offer a specific advantage. The thesis moves to a novel focus on intersectionality, which is crucial.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a **Score: 7**. This is based on the following rationale:

*   The thesis tackles a relevant and important problem, showing significant novelty in its methods and addressing both classification and generation. The introduction of different metrics and methodologies like counterfactuals, visual heatmaps, curriculum based learning for TTI models is beneficial, and can serve as a great contribution in future as well.
*   The focus on intersectionality is particularly valuable, and the development of BiasConnect and BiasGraph demonstrates a commitment to addressing a gap in existing research.
*   However, as mentioned before, the reliance on strong assumptions, subjectivity in certain components, and limited assessment of the overall approaches severely hinders its generalizability. Future evaluations on robust, real world datasets are a must for this framework.
*   The individual components are well-structured, but the level of integration between them limits a full understanding of how these components can be incorporated to work in a real-world framework.

This score acknowledges the thesis's strong theoretical foundations, methodological innovations, and potential for impact, while also reflecting the limitations and areas where further research is needed.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lattice Random Walk Discretisations of Stochastic Differential Equations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20883v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Duffield, Maxwell Aifer, Denis Melanson, Zach Belateche, Patrick J. Coles
- **Abstract**: We introduce a lattice random walk discretisation scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs) that samples binary or ternary increments at each step, suppressing complex drift and diffusion computations to simple 1 or 2 bit random values. This approach is a significant departure from traditional floating point discretisations and offers several advantages; including compatibility with stochastic computing architectures that avoid floating-point arithmetic in place of directly manipulating the underlying probability distribution of a bitstream, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantisation errors, and handling of non-Lipschitz drifts. We prove weak convergence and demonstrate the advantages through experiments on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel lattice random walk (LRW) discretization scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs). This scheme differs significantly from traditional floating-point discretization methods.  Instead of directly approximating drift and diffusion with floating-point operations, it samples binary or ternary increments at each step, effectively mapping complex computations to simple 1- or 2-bit random values. This approach offers several advantages, including compatibility with stochastic computing architectures, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantization errors, and handling of non-Lipschitz drifts. The authors prove the weak convergence of their scheme and demonstrate its advantages through experiments on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the LRW discretization itself.  The idea of mapping the complex drift and diffusion calculations onto a very simple binary/ternary sampling process is a significant departure from existing techniques.  While discretizing state space is not new, doing so in a way that directly enables stochastic computing implementations and simplifies the sampling procedure this drastically is novel.  The connection to stochastic computing and exploiting bit-level manipulations is a key contribution. The paper also makes a notable contribution in formalizing the recasting of Flow-Matching diffusion models as SDE's, providing an analytical framework for applying LRW in this domain.

*   **Significance:**  The potential significance comes from several factors:

    *   **Stochastic Computing:**  If successfully implemented on specialized stochastic computing hardware, this approach could lead to substantial speedups in SDE simulation, particularly for computationally intensive tasks like diffusion model inference. This is because it directly utilizes the probability distribution by manipulating bitstreams, avoiding floating point computations entirely.
    *   **Quantization Robustness:**  The inherent robustness to quantization errors is valuable, especially in the context of large neural networks used in modern SDE applications, where reducing precision can lead to significant savings in resources.
    *   **Non-Lipschitz Drifts:** The ability to handle non-Lipschitz drifts is a substantial advantage, as these are common in many relevant applications.  While other methods exist to handle these, the LRW provides a unique and potentially more efficient approach.
    *   **Diffusion Models:** The application of the LRW discretization to state-of-the-art diffusion models is significant, as it showcases the scalability and potential of the method in a high-dimensional and complex setting.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the LRW discretization scheme and its advantages.
    *   The theoretical results (weak convergence proof) provide a solid foundation.
    *   The experiments demonstrate the claimed benefits, particularly in robustness to quantization and handling non-Lipschitz drifts. The diffusion model experiment showcases practical applicability.
    *   The detailed explanation of the link between the LRW and stochastic computing, with a thorough citation of relevant publications, provides crucial context.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Diagonal Diffusion Assumption:**  The limitation to diagonal diffusion matrices is a significant constraint. While the authors discuss the possibility of using the Lamperti transform, it would be valuable to see a more concrete analysis of the performance of the LRW with the transform in practice. The current requirement limits applicability.
    *   **Practical Stochastic Computing Implementation:**  The paper highlights the potential for stochastic computing, but does not provide a detailed implementation or simulation on specialized hardware.  This is a crucial step to fully realize the potential benefits and quantify the actual speedups and energy savings.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Other Discretizations:** The paper focuses primarily on the comparison to Euler-Maruyama, despite the existence of other higher order or more appropriate discretization schemes based on the details of the SDE such as BAOAB or Heun's method.
*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence several areas:

    *   **SDE Simulation:**  The LRW discretization could become a viable alternative to traditional methods, particularly for applications where stochastic computing hardware is available or quantization robustness is critical.
    *   **Stochastic Computing:**  The paper provides a concrete example of how stochastic computing can be applied to a complex problem like SDE simulation, potentially stimulating further research in this area.
    *   **Diffusion Models:**  The application to diffusion models suggests new ways to optimize and accelerate these models, which are increasingly important in machine learning and image generation.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's significant novelty and potential, tempered by its limitations. The LRW discretization is a genuinely new approach with several compelling advantages.  The clear link to stochastic computing is a key strength. However, the restriction to diagonal diffusion matrices, the lack of detailed stochastic computing implementation, and the incomplete comparison to alternative discretization methods limit its current impact. It lays the foundation for a significant contribution, but further work is needed to fully realize its potential and address the limitations. The recasting of Flow-Matching diffusion models as SDE's represents a notable contribution in formalizing this area. Were the limitation to diagonal diffusion matrices or the lack of concrete stochastic computing addressed, the paper would be graded higher.

- **Classification**: math.NA
- **Score**: 7/10

### PromptSleuth: Detecting Prompt Injection via Semantic Intent Invariance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20890v1)
- **Authors**: Mengxiao Wang, Yuxuan Zhang, Guofei Gu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into real-world applications, from virtual assistants to autonomous agents. However, their flexibility also introduces new attack vectors-particularly Prompt Injection (PI), where adversaries manipulate model behavior through crafted inputs. As attackers continuously evolve with paraphrased, obfuscated, and even multi-task injection strategies, existing benchmarks are no longer sufficient to capture the full spectrum of emerging threats. To address this gap, we construct a new benchmark that systematically extends prior efforts. Our benchmark subsumes the two widely-used existing ones while introducing new manipulation techniques and multi-task scenarios, thereby providing a more comprehensive evaluation setting. We find that existing defenses, though effective on their original benchmarks, show clear weaknesses under our benchmark, underscoring the need for more robust solutions. Our key insight is that while attack forms may vary, the adversary's intent-injecting an unauthorized task-remains invariant. Building on this observation, we propose PromptSleuth, a semantic-oriented defense framework that detects prompt injection by reasoning over task-level intent rather than surface features. Evaluated across state-of-the-art benchmarks, PromptSleuth consistently outperforms existing defense while maintaining comparable runtime and cost efficiency. These results demonstrate that intent-based semantic reasoning offers a robust, efficient, and generalizable strategy for defending LLMs against evolving prompt injection threats.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PromptSleuth, a semantic-oriented defense framework designed to detect prompt injection attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs). Prompt injection (PI) attacks manipulate model behavior through crafted inputs, and the paper argues that existing defenses, relying on surface-level cues, are easily bypassed by evolving attack strategies.  The core idea is that, despite varied attack forms, the attacker's intent – injecting an unauthorized task – remains invariant. PromptSleuth analyzes the semantic relationship between intended tasks and injected tasks to identify and filter out malicious prompts. The paper constructs a comprehensive benchmark, PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH, extending existing datasets with diverse and realistic attack variants, including multi-task scenarios.  Evaluations demonstrate that PromptSleuth outperforms existing defenses while maintaining comparable efficiency across multiple LLM backends. The paper also presents ablation studies justifying the design choices and emphasizing the importance of semantic reasoning over syntactic cues.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing prompt injection defenses and the need for more robust solutions, providing a strong rationale for its work.
    *   **Novelty of Approach:**  The semantic-based approach is a significant step forward. Shifting the focus from syntactic patterns to task-level intent provides a more generalizable and adaptable defense against evolving attacks. The decomposition of the detection process into summarization, relationship graph generation, and clustering is also a well-structured and effective approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The construction of PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH is a valuable contribution.  It addresses a real gap in the field by offering a more diverse and challenging evaluation setting compared to existing benchmarks. The inclusion of multi-task scenarios is particularly important. The dataset and code are open-sourced.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of PromptSleuth over existing defenses across various LLM backends. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the impact of different design choices. The study of the performance across different models (GPT-4, GPT-5) is important.
    *   **Practicality:** The design focuses on server-side deployment and API compatibility, making it more practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Exploration of a complex topic such as memorization of LLMs and its impact of PI attacks.**
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** A key dependency on large LLMs for semantic analysis is a double-edged sword. While this enables effective reasoning, it also inherits the limitations of LLMs themselves, such as potential biases, hallucinations, and computational cost. The paper acknowledges this dependency, but the dependence on expensive APIs is still a significant limitation for widespread adoption.
    *   **Abstraction Granularity:** The effectiveness hinges on the granularity of task summarization and the accuracy of the detector LLM in determining semantic relationships. Overly broad or narrow task definitions could lead to false positives or negatives. A deeper discussion of how to optimize this granularity would be beneficial. While the paper touches this topic in terms of potential future work, the potential difficulty for implementing such optimizations is not reflected in a high score.
    *   **Limited Scope of Stored Prompt Injection mitigation:** the exploration of stored prompt injection is rather limited in scope, therefore it cannot be considered a relevant contribution.
    *   **Open Source promise:** the paper promises to contribute to the open source community by releasing the dataset and the defense; however the authors seem to have failed to release this as of November 11th, 2024.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   PromptSleuth represents a significant advancement in the field of prompt injection defense. Its semantic-based approach offers a more robust and generalizable solution than existing methods.
    *   The benchmark will likely become a valuable resource for evaluating future defenses.
    *   The paper's insights could inspire new research directions focused on understanding and mitigating the underlying intent of adversarial prompts.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper demonstrates significant novelty by shifting the focus of PI defenses to semantic understanding. PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH is a strong contribution, addressing a genuine gap and enabling more robust evaluations. However, the reliance on LLMs with expensive APIs is a key weakness, limiting its practicality, and the open source components are not yet available. I am deducting points for reliance on expensive APIs, the limited scope of stored prompt injection analysis, and the lack of open source promise being fulfilled. The current reliance on expensive LLM APIs for defense is expensive, and potentially non-sustainable given the ever-growing size of LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Uneven Impact of Post-Training Quantization in Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20893v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Marie, Atsushi Fujita
- **Abstract**: Quantization is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained hardware, but its implications for multilingual tasks remain underexplored. We conduct the first large-scale evaluation of post-training quantization (PTQ) on machine translation across 55 languages using five LLMs ranging from 1.7B to 70B parameters. Our analysis reveals that while 4-bit quantization often preserves translation quality for high-resource languages and large models, significant degradation occurs for low-resource and typologically diverse languages, particularly in 2-bit settings. We compare four quantization techniques (AWQ, BitsAndBytes, GGUF, and AutoRound), showing that algorithm choice and model size jointly determine robustness. GGUF variants provide the most consistent performance, even at 2-bit precision. Additionally, we quantify the interactions between quantization, decoding hyperparameters, and calibration languages, finding that language-matched calibration offers benefits primarily in low-bit scenarios. Our findings offer actionable insights for deploying multilingual LLMs for machine translation under quantization constraints, especially in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale evaluation of post-training quantization (PTQ) on machine translation (MT) using multilingual large language models (LLMs). It explores the impact of different quantization techniques (AWQ, BitsAndBytes, GGUF, AutoRound) and bitwidths (2-bit and 4-bit) across 55 languages. The key findings highlight that quantization can significantly degrade translation quality, especially for smaller models and low-resource languages. The study also investigates the interaction between quantization, decoding hyperparameters, and calibration datasets, providing insights for practical deployment of quantized multilingual LLMs. It finds GGUF variants to be more consistent in performance and language-matched calibration to be beneficial at extreme quantizations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper makes a notable contribution by providing the first *large-scale*, systematic evaluation of PTQ specifically in the context of multilingual MT.  While previous studies have looked at quantization or multilingual models in isolation, this study's comprehensive analysis across a wide range of languages, model sizes, and quantization techniques is a valuable addition to the field. The investigation of interactions between quantization, decoding strategies, and calibration sets is also a novel aspect.

**Significance:**  The significance of the paper lies in its practical implications for deploying multilingual LLMs in resource-constrained environments.  The identification of specific vulnerabilities (e.g., degradation of low-resource languages with aggressive quantization) allows practitioners to make informed decisions about model selection, quantization techniques, and hyperparameter tuning. The detailed analysis offers actionable insights for developing more robust and efficient MT systems for a wider range of languages and contexts.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-scale and comprehensive evaluation:** The study's strength lies in its comprehensive evaluation across a wide variety of languages, model sizes, and quantization methods.
*   **Practical insights:** The paper provides practical insights into the trade-offs between model size, quantization level, and translation quality, enabling practitioners to make informed decisions about deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments.
*   **Investigating interaction effects:** The analysis of how quantization interacts with decoding hyperparameters and calibration languages is a valuable contribution.
*   **Rigorous methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined experimental setup and clear evaluation metrics.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope of decoding strategies/prompt engineering:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of using a fixed prompt and decoding strategy. Exploring a broader range of these could reveal more nuanced interactions with quantization.
*   **Metric limitations:** The reliance on COMET, while effective for system ranking, has limitations in terms of interpretability and cross-lingual comparability of absolute scores.  While the authors acknowledge and discuss these issues, more diverse evaluation metrics (especially human evaluations on a subset) could further strengthen the findings.
*   **Focus on weight-only PTQ:**  While understandable given the complexities of LLMs, focusing solely on weight-only PTQ limits the scope.  Exploring activation quantization or more advanced mixed-precision approaches could reveal further insights.
*   **Uneven linguistic coverage:**  While the study covers 55 languages, the depth of analysis and detailed reporting focuses on only a small subset (6). Expanding the reporting on a wider range of language families could strengthen the conclusions about the impact of linguistic diversity.
*   **Choice of models:** The paper focuses on Qwen and Llama models. While they are prevalent, evaluating other models and architectures specifically optimized for multilingual MT would be valuable.
*   **Lack of statistical significance tests**: It would be beneficial to statistically validate the differences between quantized models and their original versions to guarantee the robustness of observations.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in several ways:

*   **Motivating multilingual-aware quantization techniques:** The findings can motivate the development of quantization methods that are specifically designed to mitigate the degradation of low-resource languages.
*   **Guiding practical deployment of quantized LLMs:** The paper provides practical guidance for practitioners who are deploying multilingual LLMs in real-world applications.
*   **Encouraging further research on interaction effects:** The analysis of interaction effects between quantization, decoding strategies, and calibration sets can inspire further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multilingual MT by providing a comprehensive and systematic evaluation of PTQ. The findings have practical implications for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments, and the analysis of interaction effects offers valuable insights for future research. The large-scale nature of the study, combined with its practical relevance, makes it a significant contribution. However, the limitations in decoding strategy exploration, metric limitations, linguistic coverage and model selection keep this from being a truly exceptional contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Language-Enhanced Mobile Manipulation for Efficient Object Search in Indoor Environments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20899v1)
- **Authors**: Liding Zhang, Zeqi Li, Kuanqi Cai, Qian Huang, Zhenshan Bing, Alois Knoll
- **Abstract**: Enabling robots to efficiently search for and identify objects in complex, unstructured environments is critical for diverse applications ranging from household assistance to industrial automation. However, traditional scene representations typically capture only static semantics and lack interpretable contextual reasoning, limiting their ability to guide object search in completely unfamiliar settings. To address this challenge, we propose a language-enhanced hierarchical navigation framework that tightly integrates semantic perception and spatial reasoning. Our method, Goal-Oriented Dynamically Heuristic-Guided Hierarchical Search (GODHS), leverages large language models (LLMs) to infer scene semantics and guide the search process through a multi-level decision hierarchy. Reliability in reasoning is achieved through the use of structured prompts and logical constraints applied at each stage of the hierarchy. For the specific challenges of mobile manipulation, we introduce a heuristic-based motion planner that combines polar angle sorting with distance prioritization to efficiently generate exploration paths. Comprehensive evaluations in Isaac Sim demonstrate the feasibility of our framework, showing that GODHS can locate target objects with higher search efficiency compared to conventional, non-semantic search strategies. Website and Video are available at: https://drapandiger.github.io/GODHS
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Language-Enhanced Mobile Manipulation for Efficient Object Search in Indoor Environments":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GODHS (Goal-Oriented Dynamically Heuristic-Guided Hierarchical Search), a novel framework for mobile robots to efficiently search for objects in unfamiliar indoor environments. GODHS leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to provide semantic reasoning and guide the search process through a five-level hierarchy (Scene -> Room -> Carrier -> Feature -> Item).  The system uses structured prompts and logical constraints to ensure reliable LLM-based reasoning. It also incorporates a heuristic-based motion planner that combines polar angle sorting and distance prioritization for efficient exploration pose generation. Experiments conducted in Isaac Sim demonstrate that GODHS outperforms conventional non-semantic search strategies in terms of search efficiency (lower search rates).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of LLMs into a hierarchical search framework for mobile manipulation is a significant contribution.  While previous work has explored LLMs for robotic tasks, GODHS stands out by its systematic hierarchical approach and the incorporation of structured prompting to enhance reliability. The heuristic-based motion planner, combining polar angle sorting and distance prioritization, also contributes to improved efficiency. However, individual components like hierarchical decomposition or heuristic planning, are not entirely novel in isolation. The novelty arises from the synergistic combination of these components with LLM-based reasoning, guided by structured prompts, specifically tailored for efficient object search.

*   **Significance:**  Enabling robots to efficiently search and retrieve objects in unstructured environments is crucial for various applications, including household assistance and industrial automation. By effectively leveraging LLMs for semantic reasoning and combining it with efficient motion planning, GODHS has the potential to significantly improve the performance of mobile robots in these tasks. Demonstrating this capability in a simulation environment like Isaac Sim is an important step.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Hierarchical Search:** The hierarchical decomposition of the search problem into multiple levels allows for efficient pruning of the search space, reducing the overall search effort.
    *   **LLM-based Semantic Reasoning:** Integrating LLMs provides the robot with commonsense knowledge and contextual understanding, enabling it to infer likely object locations and prioritize search areas.
    *   **Structured Prompting:** Using structured prompts and logical constraints ensures the reliability and consistency of the LLM's reasoning, mitigating the risk of hallucination or incorrect predictions.
    *   **Heuristic-based Motion Planning:** The proposed motion planner efficiently generates and prioritizes exploration poses, reducing redundant travel and execution time.
    *   **Simulation Results:** Simulation results show superior performance compared to non-semantic methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulation Dependence:** The experiments are conducted in Isaac Sim, which may not perfectly reflect the complexities and uncertainties of real-world environments.  Transitioning to physical robot experiments is crucial.
    *   **LLM Limitations:** The performance of GODHS relies heavily on the capabilities of the LLM. LLM performance varies and is impacted by biases. The reliance on the reasoning abilities of Qwen2.5-7B and GPT-40 also exposes the framework to potential limitations in their commonsense knowledge and the computational resources required. Future research can focus on more efficient models with similar reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Failure Modes:** The identified failure modes, such as hardware limitations and semantic ambiguity, highlight the challenges of deploying GODHS in real-world scenarios. Additional work needs to be done to address these limitations.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The comparison with full coverage and random search is not strong. Comparison with more sophisticated, though not LLM-based, approaches would be more informative.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The GODHS framework offers a promising direction for future research in mobile manipulation and object search. The combination of LLM-based reasoning with hierarchical search and efficient motion planning is likely to inspire further developments in this field. Successfully transitioning the framework to physical robots and addressing the identified limitations could significantly impact real-world applications. Further work should investigate adapting the framework to different types of robots and environments. Additionally, evaluating the framework's robustness to noisy sensor data and dynamic environments is important.

**Justification for Score:**

GODHS presents a novel and promising approach to object search in indoor environments, leveraging LLMs for semantic reasoning and combining it with hierarchical search and efficient motion planning. The simulation results demonstrate improved performance compared to baseline methods. However, the simulation-based evaluation and dependence on the LLM's capabilities, as well as the relatively weak baselines, limit the immediate impact. Future work to transition to real-world environments and address identified failure modes is essential to fully realize the framework's potential. Considering these factors, a score of 7 is assigned, indicating a solid contribution with significant potential for future impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Research Challenges in Relational Database Management Systems for LLM Queries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20912v1)
- **Authors**: Kerem Akillioglu, Anurag Chakraborty, Sairaj Voruganti, M. Tamer Özsu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have become essential for applications such as text summarization, sentiment analysis, and automated question-answering. Recently, LLMs have also been integrated into relational database management systems to enhance querying and support advanced data processing. Companies such as Amazon, Databricks, Google, and Snowflake offer LLM invocation directly within SQL, denoted as LLM queries, to boost data insights. However, open-source solutions currently have limited functionality and poor performance. In this work, we present an early exploration of two open-source systems and one enterprise platform, using five representative queries to expose functional, performance, and scalability limits in today's SQL-invoked LLM integrations. We identify three main issues: enforcing structured outputs, optimizing resource utilization, and improving query planning. We implemented initial solutions and observed improvements in accommodating LLM powered SQL queries. These early gains demonstrate that tighter integration of LLM+DBMS is the key to scalable and efficient processing of LLM queries.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary**
